| P51 | C6 (loop C round C12, pre-registered; relaunch of P46) | does our L-GATr (Lorentz-equivariant tagger trained here, final checkpoint model_run0.pt) respond to the jet axis at fixed invariant mass? Read-out d = ℓ_W − ℓ_Z (W logit minus Z logit); R = Σ_jets Δd(edit) / Σ_jets Δd(E4), where E4 scales every particle's angle about the stored axis so that M2 = Σ pT r² changes by what the edit predicts (a real mass change of the same predicted size). Edits: BOOST ± 0.06 (every particle's rapidity shifted by 0.06 at fixed pT and mass: an exact longitudinal boost, invariant mass unchanged, M2 about the stored axis +≈ 17 %), split into routes A (full boost), D (the 7 per-particle scalar features such as log E held at their unedited values), F (only those features changed), C (time-reference spurion (1,0,0,0) boosted too: an exact symmetry, so the numerical floor); E1m ± 2/5/10 % (one particle pair spread apart or together, then a second pair moved until the jet invariant mass is restored to 1e-13); E5 (pair move at fixed M2); PHI ± 0.06 and E6 (stored-axis move) as symmetry / plumbing checks | done (round C12); BOOST result reproduced by P51v; E1m / E5 reading corrected and the pooled-E1m exclusion not reproduced (confidence 0.5) | jobs c-p51-smoke, c-p51-run (k8s-haosu-23, not chase-ci-01; 46 min), c-p51-ana3 (CPU analysis); interp/p46_lgatr_c6.py (tag 'final' → model_run0.pt; outputs prefixed p51); results/lgatr/p51_lgatr_c6.json, figure p51_lgatr_c6.png. P51v rerun (val_5M _124, default_rng(5199), N 3,000): BOOST L-GATr R −0.0001 [−0.0018, 0.0013], ParT_full 0.820 [0.804, 0.836], ParT_kin 0.745; L-GATr − ParT_full −0.821 [−0.836, −0.805], Holm p 0.0035; E1m pooled linear L-GATr 0.055 [−0.160, 0.292]; E5 −0.026 [−0.156, 0.096] | Conclusion: Boosting every particle of a W or Z jet along the beam, which leaves the jet mass unchanged, does not change the final L-GATr's W-vs-Z score on two samples (R ≈ 0, against about 0.8 for ParT), so L-GATr does not read ParT's stored jet axis. The fixed-mass rearrangements also keep the particles' spread about their pT centre fixed, and ParT_full gives R ≈ 0 on them too, so they cannot show that L-GATr reads the invariant mass rather than the spread about its own momentum axis. That separation is still open | refines (C6 0.0 → 0.6, v2 wording; C22 note) |

Untrained vs full swap (x10-verify3u): seed 0 M0 0.45 (DAS 0.02–0.37), M2 0.39 (0.05–0.24); seed 2 M0 0.44 (0.18–0.37), M2 0.49 (0.12–0.34); trained above it in 15/18 M0/M2 cells, untrained below in 36/36; W−Z: untrained seed 0 S 0.48/0.52 vs trained 0.43/0.35, seed 2 h5 k1 0.69 vs 0.30 | corrected reading (X10v): subspaces of 1–2 dims at S, h4, h5 reproduce the call changes of M0 and M2 input edits on 0.66–0.87 of call-changing pairs (random ≤ 0.14), with the range clip as part of the intervention. Against the full-swap baseline: M0 +0.5 (S, h4), M2 +0.05 to +0.17, W−Z +0.15 to +0.30 at h4/h5 (k ≥ 2), not above at S. The "Δ" subspaces reproduce the response to a global pT rescale, not an angle-only edit of the same Δ (that one follows the M2 subspace), so Δ is not shown as one variable at any stage. Random subspaces do not separate trained from untrained networks; the full-swap baseline does for M0 and M2, not for W−Z. Conclusion: swapping one or two learned directions of JEDI-linear's object layer between two jets changes its decision as an edit of total pT or of the pT-weighted squared spread about the axis does, on most decision changes, far above random directions and, for total pT, far above simply giving the jet the other jet's class. The combination ½log(M0·M2) is not found as one separate quantity: the directions found for it follow a pT rescale, while the same change made with angles runs through the spread directions. How much the spread directions add beyond the full-swap baseline is small (+0.05 to +0.17) | refines B2m (variables exist as subspaces; named-formula basis not needed) and A4 (M0, M2 at S); weakens the A4 product / stage clause (Δ not a separate variable); untrained control: random-subspace test not sufficient |

Bases: native, 20 raw rotations (primary null), 10 z-scored rotations, PCA, ReLU SAE 2×/4× (dev FVU ≤ 0.10). Named = held-out R² ≥ 0.9 for ≤ 8-term MARS on 97 rank-window observables. Edit test: pT or angles × 1.1 in the top term's window, slope CI ⊂ [0.8, 1.2]. Accuracy JEDI 80.87 %, Deep Sets 80.57 %, PFN 81.30 %. SAE − native: JEDI S −0.63 / −0.58, h4 −0.45 / −0.44, Deep Sets −0.22 / −0.25 (Holm 0.028); PFN 0.00. Edit pass: JEDI S 7/19 (S0 0.85, S1 1.16, S29 0.91, S40, S43, S50, S51; S19 0.13, S30 0.48 fail) vs rotations 0.22; mismatched-formula control 0/15. Median R² native: JEDI S 0.955, h4 0.906, Deep Sets 0.663, PFN 0.694. **X11v rerun** pass 1 (sample(4000, 101/103)): JEDI S 0.789 vs 200 rotations max 0.632 (p 0.005); h4 0.564 vs 40 rotations max 0.364; generic 380-observable library 0.737 vs 50 rotations max 0.579; SAE − native −0.63; among named directions native vs rotated pass the edit test at 0.47 vs 0.50 (p 0.62). Pass 2 (fit sample(3000, 401), test sample(3000, 409), 14,141 jets; 80.84 %): JEDI S 0.789 (same 15 units) vs 50 rotations mean 0.397, max 0.526 (p 0.020); Deep Sets 0.288 vs 10 rotations max 0.119. Untrained float networks: JEDI architecture 0/56 and 1/57 named, Deep Sets 0/46, rotations ≤ 0.065 | On a fixed library of rank-window pT/angle observables, JEDI-linear's S units are named at 0.79 on three samples, above every one of 250 random rotations; h4 0.55–0.56 above 40 rotations; SAE and PCA directions are named less often in every model. Deep Sets pooled units also beat their rotations (0.29); the PFN latent does not. The edit-test excess over rotations follows from the naming excess (named native vs rotated 0.47 vs 0.50). Untrained architectures have almost no nameable units, so readability needs training; whether the basis preference is learned or architectural is not decided (control at floor). Intervals hold formulas fixed. Conclusion: JEDI-linear's pooled object units can each be written as a short formula of pT and angular-distance sums over pT-rank windows for 15 of 19 units, on three independent jet samples, while no random mixing of the same units reaches that rate. A sparse autoencoder makes the layer less, not more, readable by this measure. Deep Sets units also prefer their own basis at a lower rate; the PFN latent does not. Open: whether the unit basis is preferred because of training or because of the per-particle ReLU-sum architecture, and how much the SAE result depends on the additive hinge scorer | supports A11 (new A11b, 0.75); supports A3 (S units nameable); weakness 4 addressed for JEDI-linear; basis preference not unique to JEDI-linear (Deep Sets) |

Network-level permutation: radial hinge all usable HGQ vs float p 0.048, primary arm p 0.17; flow(T) p 0.048 | corrected reading (X12v): the translation W→Z response removed by holding Σ pT r² fixed recurs in 4 of 5 usable retrained HGQ networks (HGQ-150 s0 passes only because its unedited rate equals the threshold). Float networks give 2–3× lower W→Z under T, but relative to a real widening with the same M0 and M2 they respond as much (0.69–1.30 vs 1.02–1.38, p 0.24) and to a real W→Z mass shift as strongly; the lower W→Z is where the float networks put the Z boundary for ~105 GeV-equivalent jets, not weaker use of the uncentred spread. No retrained network reproduces the official 13|14 swap response (0.20–0.90 vs 5.18), although all quantized ones form zero-bit slot masks. Purity follows from the 13–20-entry sparsity HGQ produces. Radial hinge objects are more frequent with quantization in every network; network-level p 0.048 (all usable) / 0.17 (primary). Conclusion: retrained copies of JEDI-linear, with or without quantization-aware training, read the jet's mass through the pT-weighted spread about the stored axis, as the official network does, so that behaviour belongs to the architecture and inputs, not to one checkpoint. The official network's strong response at the 14-hardest-particle boundary does not appear in any retrained copy, so it is specific to that checkpoint or to its much longer training. "Pure" first-layer cuts follow from how few weights quantization-aware training keeps. The retrained quantized copies are 1–4 points less accurate and trained about 10× fewer epochs | refines A10m (mass reading shared by HGQ and float retrainings; "weaker without quantization" not supported); weakens A1 generality (window effect specific to the official checkpoint); A1q reworded (sparsity, not purity); A3 radial excess with quantization at network-level p 0.048 |

Per network, aug accuracy loss up to 1.13 points (Deep Sets s1) | corrected reading (X9wv): PFN, EFN and Deep Sets trained on stored-axis inputs send 0.35–0.71 of W jets to Z under a 0.06 rad rigid translation; the same architectures trained with translation augmentation (\|c\| ≤ 0.08 rad) are at the rotation-null level (≤ 0.012) at ≤ 1.13 points accuracy per network; recentred networks give exactly 0 by construction. A 0.12 rad translation sends 0.51–0.84 of the augmented networks' W jets to Z, so augmentation suppresses the response only for offsets inside the trained range; X9w's "mass read about the jet's own centroid" holds only for recentred networks. All networks respond to dilM2. In retrained JEDI-linear HGQ-50, aug reduced the 0.06 rad flow to the null level in 1 of 3 seeds; the other two keep 0.38–0.50 and lost 6–14 accuracy points, so architecture vs under-training is not separated. The representation and interaction 2×2 effects are fixed by how recentred inputs are built; only stored vs aug is an empirical test. Conclusion: in three standard set networks the uncentred mass response comes from the training data: when the networks are trained on jets randomly shifted by up to 0.08 rad they stop responding to such shifts, at a cost of about one accuracy point at most. They do not learn a shift-proof mass, since a larger shift (0.12 rad) still moves most W jets to Z. Whether the same holds for JEDI-linear is open, because its augmented retrainings lost several accuracy points and two of three kept the response | refines A10m (input-format / training-data origin in PFN, EFN, Deep Sets within the augmented range; new claim A10r 0.55); JEDI-linear part unresolved |

JEDI vs F1 under A′ McNemar p 0.60 (vs F2 p 0.27). Accuracy drop A′ vs rot: JEDI 1.45 [0.97, 1.90], F1 1.14 [0.65, 1.61], F0 0.00. τ21 and D2 add 0.22 and 0.42 points of accuracy over mass and radial features (78.71 → 78.93 → 79.13 %). rot2 − rot null for JEDI −0.59 [−0.99, −0.21] points (rotation-draw spread ±0.5 points, not in the jet bootstrap) | corrected reading (E24v): under the scramble JEDI-linear changes 7.1–7.2 % of calls (rotation 4.0–4.3 %), the same share as a boosted tree that uses τ21 (6.9 %, McNemar p 0.60), because prong shape adds little beyond mass and radial shape on these jets; the uncalibrated 20–40 % alternative is not excluded by this test. JEDI's excess over rotation (2.8 points) lies between the mass-and-radial tree (1.1) and the τ21 tree (6.4), and its accuracy loss (1.45 points) equals the τ21 tree's. Latent results hold: the named radial S objects change ≤ 0.19 sd; the excess passes mostly through the context-only units S58, S31, which read the jet context g. Deep Sets and PFN change 7.3–8.7 % of calls, so call-level near-invariance does not separate JEDI-linear from them; at the latent level JEDI's S moves more under scramble than under rotation (0.47 vs 0.21 of its spread), Deep Sets and PFN less. Conclusion: randomising each particle's direction around the jet axis destroys the two-prong pattern of W jets and changes about 7 % of JEDI-linear's calls; a classifier built explicitly on the two-prong variable τ21 changes the same share, because prong shape adds little beyond mass and radial shape here. So this test cannot tell whether JEDI-linear uses prong structure; its extra change over rotation and its accuracy loss sit between a classifier that ignores prong shape and one that uses it. The named radial objects barely move; the change runs through two units that read only a jet-level summary | refines A3 (radial S objects ≤ 0.19 sd under the scramble); weakens the planned object-vs-Deep-Sets separation at call level (Deep Sets, PFN alike); new claims A3z (0.6) and A13 (azimuthal dependence at fixed moments, 0.5) |

Larger GBDT / MLP (4–124× bits) 5–12 points higher trans agreement | corrected reading (B6v): at about 13 kbit the blind program agrees with the networks' call changes under the 0.06 rad translation about as often as a tree, GBDT or MLP of at most the same bits selected on dev jets: −1.3 to +1.7 points over three samples in JEDI-linear and PFN, +0.2 (Deep Sets) and +1.6 (transformer) on B6 jets; with test-edit (oracle) selection the baseline is 0–2.4 points higher in all four. The 2.8–8.1-point lead over B6's single lr 0.2 GBDT comes from an under-fitted baseline and depends on requiring the same new call (that GBDT has higher flipped-set Jaccard). Under the M2-held translation the program leads in JEDI-linear on all three samples (+1.7 to +2.0, Holm ≤ 6e-5) and in the transformer on B6 jets (+1.7), not in PFN or Deep Sets; every surrogate ≤ 14 kbit is within about 3 points of never-flip there. Conclusion: a short list of if-statements on sums over pT-ranked particle windows follows how four different networks change their calls under a small jet shift about as well as tuned boosted trees or decision trees of the same size, with differences up to about 2 points either way depending on the jet sample. The earlier lead came from comparing against one under-fitted boosted-tree setting. Untrained seeds 3–8 constant | Corrected reading (E25v): the 16-feature first layer reads angles only through ΔR (10/17 live cuts, 8 mixed with pT) and one rotated-frame coordinate, zero weight on Δη, Δφ, η, φ; the share of mixed cuts equals random placement among active units (E25's "more mixed than random placement" withdrawn). A 0.06 rad translation turns 48–49 % of true W jets into Z (rotation 1.3–1.5 %), carried entirely by the ΔR column (ΔR held 1.2–1.4 %), close to an M2-matched ΔR dilation (0.98 at 0.03 rad, 1.05–1.07 at 0.06; "equals" withdrawn); an M2-preserving ΔR scramble gives 3.1 %, so the response follows Σ pT ΔR² about the stored axis; W→Z not monotone in shift size; no untrained baseline (8/9 redraws constant). Route (a) mass via ΔR fits; (b) half-planes and (c) absolute coordinates excluded. Conclusion: The more accurate 16-feature JEDI-linear does not build angular information from half-plane cuts; its first layer reads each particle's distance from the stored jet axis and ignores the separate Δη and Δφ inputs. Its mass-like response is still not recentred: moving all particles 0.06 rad off the axis turns about half of W jets into Z calls, through the distance column alone, and the response follows the pT-weighted spread of the particles about the axis. There is no usable untrained baseline, and where the mass variable forms in the later layers was not tested | refines A1x (half-plane construction specific to 3-feature inputs; new A1f 0.75), A4 (0.92 → 0.93), A10m (0.85 → 0.87) |

The quadrupole name is not a complete causal account of S58 (a 180° turn, which leaves quadrupole sums unchanged, moves S58 by 40 % of its 90° response; named θ explains 10 %, exact θ 72 %). S31's edit response is shaped by the per-slot l3 quantizer. S-level R² (0.79 / 0.63) limited by naming and quantization. Conclusion: The two summary units that carry JEDI-linear's context response do not look at individual particles; they read only the jet-level summary, which is built from plain counts of particles on either side of fixed lines. Their thresholds follow how far the particle-count centre sits from the axis (S31) and how elongated the particle-count distribution is (S58), fixed-frame shapes rather than rotation-invariant jet quantities. The elongation name does not account for all of S58's response when the jet is turned by 180°, and the network's per-slot rounding shapes S31's response | refines A3z (naming done; new A3c 0.65), A13 (fixed-frame context consistent with azimuth dependence) |

Instrumented, a1/a2 is 0.77 at S (equal to S-type radial sums, 0.69), 0.97 at h4, 0.86–0.87 at h5 and z (0.90–0.95 with a \|c\|² term), undetermined at the margin. E23w's "0.4–0.8 at every stage", "toward 1 from h4", "rises with \|c\|" and "odd shrinks 8–14× on bulk" are estimator properties; E23v's 1.34–1.82 odd gain not reproduced. Product test: no z unit or margin outside a wide random-pair band (not rerun); stage of any product not located. Conclusion: When naturally off-centre jets are shifted sideways, JEDI-linear weighs the part of the change that depends on the jet's centre offset about as much as the part that depends on the shift size from its dense layers on, once a measurement bias is removed; only the first summed stage weighs it less, as its radial sums predict. Other S units that carry the same S-level odd power pass on about twice as much downstream, so most of the odd response, including the margin's, goes through the particle-reading S units. With M2 held, moving only the hard 14 changes the margin about 2× more than moving only the soft particles (on top/gluon-dominated feasible jets; 1.03 without the hold). The odd response at S–z follows Σr² rather than M2, mainly because a shift changes Σr² 9× more. Conclusion: When all particles of a jet are shifted, the part of the network's response that depends on the shift direction passes only partly through the two context units that sum particle counts on each side of the axis (S31 and S58): about a quarter in the later layers and none in the final W-versus-Z score. Other second-round units that respond equally strongly pass on more of their response, so most of the direction-dependent response goes through the units that read individual particles. Which of those units carry it, and how it behaves on W and Z jets specifically, is still open | weakens the A4d/A3c link (S31/S58 majority route excluded); A4d 0.6 (=), A3c 0.65 (=), A4n 0.5 (=) |

Jobs e25w-run, e25w-run2 (--nrand 5000), e25w-dose, e25w-base (1 CPU / 2 GiB, peak 1.3–1.6 GiB), e25w-verify (852 s, RSS 1.70 GiB); interp/e25w_jedi16_mass.py, e25w_plot.py, e25w_verify.py; results/jedi/e25w_form*.json, e25w_dose.json, e25w_base.json, e25w.png, e25w_verify.json; inbox/merged/E25w_report.md, E25w_verify.md | Corrected reading (E25wv): the 16-feature net has separate 2-dim causal subspaces for M0 and M2 at S whose IIA declines toward the logits; the same DAS on the 3-feature net gives the same picture for M0/M2, and the nets differ only in D (rises S → h4 in 3f, falls in 16f; no paired test). Where M0/M2 first appear (P → S) is untested; the "P full swap" swaps only the g context route. Under translation W calls pass through Z (peak 0.87 at 0.045 rad) into top and q/g; an M2-matched ΔR dilation reproduces the aggregate W→Z count, dilations matched on Σ pT r or Σ r do not; per jet 3–26 % of calls differ. The trained flow exceeds all 10 non-trained baselines (p 0.091, the n = 10 floor). Conclusion: In the more accurate 16-feature network, the total pT and the pT-weighted spread of particles about the jet axis are each held as a separate internal direction already in the second pooled layer, just as in the 3-feature network; the two networks differ only in how the combined mass-like quantity develops in the next layer. Shifting all particles off the axis turns W jets first into Z and then into top or quark/gluon calls, and a stretch of the particle distances that changes the spread by the same amount gives the same number of W→Z changes, while stretches matched on other radial sums do not. R_rad near 1 is mostly a property of the edit (true-mass read-out 0.80, axis-moment read-out 1.00); across networks R_rad ≈ 0.80 + 0.33 R, so R is close to the share of the log-m read-out tied to the stored axis, the rest from shift-invariant quantities (pairwise distances or centroid recentring, not separated). The response differs from rotation in all 56 (negative in 2). Family explains R among JEDI-linear, PFN, EFN, Deep Sets (p 0.006), not when attention models are included (p 0.33). Seed differences below about 0.15 are not reproducible. Conclusion: Every one of the 56 trained networks reacts to a shift of the whole jet that leaves the true mass unchanged, so none of them computes the invariant mass alone. How strongly they react matches how much of their mass estimate is measured from the stored jet axis rather than from quantities that do not move with a shift, such as distances between particles. That share is set by the network type for the set-based networks, but in transformer-type networks it differs between training runs of the same design, by a factor of about six in one case. Which internal parts compute the shift-independent share, and whether it is particle-pair distances or a recentring, is still open | refines A10 (=), A10m (=); new claim A10s (0.45); X13's cancellation mechanism and "architecture labels explain nothing" not kept |

Jobs e26-run (1 CPU / 3 GiB, 960 s), e26-null1 (1 / 2 GiB, 622 s), e26-verify (1 / 2 GiB, 565 s); interp/e26_boost_route.py, e26_plot.py, e26_verify.py; results/jedi/e26_boost_route.json, e26_boost_route_null1.json, e26_boost_route_perjet.npz, e26_boost_route.png, e26_verify.json; inbox/merged/E26_report.md, E26_verify.md | Corrected reading (E26v): at λ 0.9 (pT −10 %, mass fixed) holding S0 (ΣpT of the 14 hardest particles) at its unboosted value removes 55–57 % of the boost's call changes, g49 51 %, a15 40–42 %, S0+S29 78–79 %, the 2-dim DAS M0/M2 subspace at S 83–85 %, which equals the best possible 2-dim linear hold (85 %). Random directions in the 19 live S coordinates reach at most 0.48 / 0.45 / 0.56 (1 / 2 / 4 dims); the named holds exceed this null (p at the draw floor), but the top-4 principal directions of S also remove 77 %. The soft-particle pT ramps (12.5–14.5 %), the a32 hold and the 11 GeV clamp (≈ 0) are excluded as the route. The h4 log m read-out does not move on average (κ ≈ 0; M0 and M2 weights equal within about 2 %), moves per jet with sd 0.05, and holding or patching its direction changes no calls; other mass-encoding h4 directions untested. At λ 1.1 M0 and M2 directions act in opposite senses (DAS M2 alone −43 %), S0 alone ≈ 0, S0+S29 51 %; single h4/h5 holds at λ 1.1 were tested against an S-level null only. The chain a15 → g49 → S0 is inferred from separate holds, not a path-specific patch. Conclusion: When a jet is boosted along its axis so that its mass stays fixed but its pT drops by 10 %, about a third of JEDI-linear's decisions change. About half of those changes disappear if the network's internal sum of particle pT is held at its pre-boost value, and most disappear if two learned directions for total pT and pT-weighted angular spread are held; the network's mass estimate itself does not move on average. So the network uses total pT as a class feature next to its mass, and that is what makes it boost-sensitive; how much of this is specific to those two named directions, rather than to any high-variance directions, is not settled. | new claim A14 (0.6); refines A3 (=), A4 (=), A4-product (=); predicted pT-ramp / 11 GeV route excluded; read-out-free M0-vs-M2 weighting at h4: equal within about 2 % |

Jobs x14-ckpt-{0..5}-b, train-x14-grid-* (GPU, 3 runs per job, 3 CPU / 9 GiB), x14-grid-*, x14-ana, x14-verify; interp/x14_axis_training.py, x14_plot.py, x14_verify.py; train/train_small.py, train/make_jobs.py; results/jedi/x14_summary.json, x14_runs/, x14_axis_training.png, x14_verify.json; inbox/merged/X14_report.md, X14_verify.md | Corrected reading (X14v): for tf-2b-d32 and linf-2b-d32-k8 at N 64 training is deterministic, so final R is a fixed function of initial weights, batch order and schedule. Changing only the init seed or only the batch order changes final R by up to 0.31–0.43; changing only the schedule length (30 vs 100 epochs, which also changes the learning-rate shape) changed it by +0.68 to +0.72 at seed 0 and −0.11 to −0.19 at seed 1. The 3 × 3 grid shares are at their exchangeable-null values (0.25 / 0.25 / 0.50; restricted p ≥ 0.25) on two samples: no additive init or order effect is detected with 9 runs per model, and the residual is not evidence of an interaction. R settles around epochs 5–20 of 30 (40–65 of 100) and changes by ≤ 0.14 after the val-loss plateau; within one architecture early R shows no detectable rank correlation with final R at n 9; final R shows no detectable correlation with val accuracy or loss. Per-run uncertainty of R across samples is 0.04–0.12, larger than the jet-bootstrap CIs. R describes a refitted last-layer read-out of log m; the network's own Z−W logit share moved the opposite way in the seed-0 schedule pair. Conclusion: In small transformer-type taggers, how much the last layer's mass code depends on the stored jet axis is not set by the starting weights; it forms in the middle of training and changes when the starting weights, the batch order or the length of training is changed, with no single factor detectably dominant in 9 runs per model. Training is exactly repeatable, so the run-to-run spread comes from the optimisation path and is not linked to accuracy. Whether this last-layer code reflects what the network's decision uses is uncertain, since the decision moved the other way in one case. | refines A10s (wording v2: training path, not seed; scope to the read-out); A10, A10m unchanged; H_init, H_data, H_drift, H_acc not supported |

Against fully random networks at the same step budget, the trained first-layer M2 swap is below the random-network median in 3f (0.48 vs 0.57) and inside the distribution in 16f (0.72 vs 0.78); E25x's stage-a q95 was set mainly by init/gauss nets that keep the trained l1 and ran 250 instead of 600 steps. In 16f the first-layer direction also reproduces calls under an edit that keeps every pT_i, M0 and M2 fixed (0.68 vs 0.72). So the first-layer M2 swap is not shown to come from training, and "M2 first appears at u or S" is not excluded. M0 at a and M0, M2 at S are above most fully random nets (rank p 0.06–0.29 with 6–15 nets; Holm ≥ 0.5). Replicated: 3f M2 rises from a to S (+0.28), 16f M2 flat from a to S with a dip at u (−0.27); D in a 1-dim subspace rises S → h4 in 3f only, not with 2 dims, and a fixed-D edit in the S coordinates changes calls almost as often as a D-changing one. Conclusion: We tried to find where in JEDI-linear the total pT and the pT-weighted angular spread of the particles first appear as separate internal quantities. A single direction in each particle's first-layer values can be swapped to reproduce a change of the spread, but networks with entirely random weights allow the same swap equally well, so this test cannot show that training placed the spread there. In the 3-feature network the swap works better at the second summed layer than at the first; where training first builds the spread remains open, and the comparisons with random networks need more random networks to reach significance. | weakens (A4c 0.65 → 0.6: S-level M0/M2 swaps not separated from fully random nets after Holm); A4-product (=), A9 (=); start of the mass chain not located |

Jobs e27-smoke, e27-run (1 CPU / 3 GiB, 2,880 s, 2,770 MiB), e27-verify (1 CPU / 2 GiB, 1,180 s); interp/e27_angularity_jedi.py, e27_plot.py, e27_verify.py; results/jedi/e27_angularity.json, e27_angularity_perjet.npz, e27_angularity.png, e27_verify.json; inbox/merged/E27_report.md, E27_verify.md | Corrected reading (E27v): with M0, M1, M2 held, changing a count-weighted (κ 0) or pT²-weighted (κ 2) angularity by 5 % moves W-called jets at the rate of a matched random reshuffle; raising M2 with one of them held moves them at the dilation's rate, so the mass response is carried by a pT-weighted (κ 1) radial quantity. Within κ 1 the output is not a function of M2 alone: with M2 held, Σ pT r^β edits move the W−Z margin by 0.35–1.6 dilation units (toward W for β ≤ 1.5, toward Z for β ≥ 2.5), and at 2 % the β ≤ 1.5 edits give dilation-level W→Z flow. Which pT-weighted combination, and whether girth (Σ pT r) enters, is not determined. M0 and M1 are held in every edit, so M2 and the jet mass move together: the design does not separate M2 from m. Conclusion: The network's mass response depends on particles being weighted by their pT, not counted equally or weighted by pT squared. Among pT-weighted angular spreads it responds to more than the squared-distance sum that enters the jet mass. The internal route is not identified: neither S0+S19 nor the 17 rank-window objects remove more than random sets of the same size, and S29, S56 respond along redundant and opposing paths. E28's "at rank 0 not separated from control" holds only for the swap control; "most of the path over unnamed objects" not kept. Conclusion: Splitting one particle into two collinear ones changes 2–4 % of JEDI-linear's calls and pushes quark calls toward gluon, more than moving the same particle without splitting it, so the network is neither collinear- nor soft-safe. The route inside the network is not identified: holding the named count and rank-window sums removes no more than holding random sets of the same size | new A15 (0.6); A1, A3 unchanged (mediation by named objects not shown) |

Jobs e26w-smoke2, e26w-run (1 CPU / 2 GiB, 2,427 s), e26w-verify (1,061 s); interp/e26w_boost_specificity.py, e26w_plot.py, e26w_verify.py; results/jedi/e26w_boost_specificity.json, e26w_boost_specificity.png, e26w_verify.json; inbox/merged/E26w_report.md, E26w_verify.md | Corrected reading (E26wv): at λ 0.9 holding S0 removes 57 % of the boost's call changes, more than PC1 (6 %), any S0-free random direction of equal variance (max 37 %, p 0.002) and any other single S unit (≤ 7.5 %). The boost's displacement at S is itself 66–74 % on S0, so a displacement-matched null re-samples S0, and "specific to S0" cannot be separated from "follows the boost displacement" at this layer. S0's change arrives 68 % via context unit g49 (a15 → g49 → S0; g49 changes no other S unit). At λ 1.1 S0 removes nothing; DAS M0+M2 55 %. Data: ΣpT of the 14 hardest separates W/Z from t at fixed mass (AUC 0.73) — consistent with the network's use, not a test of it. Conclusion: When a jet's pT is lowered by 10 % at fixed mass, the network's response passes mainly through its total-pT sum, which receives most of its change through one context unit summarising the whole jet. Directions that avoid total pT and have the same spread in data undo far less. Whether total pT is singled out, or simply is where the boost moves the network, cannot be separated at this layer | A14 v2 (0.6 → 0.65); E26w's "not singled out" reading and "artefact excluded" wording not kept |

The context round compresses to 1–2 g units. **Conclusion:** An automated search that keeps only the connections the output depends on picks out all the hand-named first-layer particle cuts and nearly all hand-named jet quantities, and recovers about 95 % of the accuracy above chance with about 115–119 of 186 active units. The hand program with all output-layer units matches the search at equal size only when removed units are set to their average; when they take values from a jet of the confusable class, the search's circuit recovers 0.05 more. Because the hand names cover almost every active unit at the cut and jet-quantity layers, the agreement shows consistency with an output-driven search, not that the names are preferred over other descriptions of the same units | refines A2 (context compressible to g4 + g49/g55 at 0.02–0.04 F); consistent with A1, A3; new A16 (0.7) |

Job e30-run 1 CPU / 3 GiB, 1,064 s (2 GiB attempt killed in data load) | S1 is one hinge sum over its whole range; S29 except its weakest tenth; S0 the same quantity at a gain that varies. The weak range is the same quantity with a different offset, not a second set of objects. The negative side of z0/z7 continues the same mass read past the window. **Conclusion:** The second-round sums tested respond to a one-particle edit in their weakest jets by the same multiple of the formula prediction as in their strongest, so each behaves like one jet observable over its range; the exceptions are S29's weakest tenth (about 0.8 of the prediction, cause unknown) and S0's strongest tenth (a smaller gain). For two mass-window neurons the negative side measures how far the jet's mass lies beyond the window, and a ±5 % mass edit moves it accordingly; the ReLU discards it. Whether the mass neurons' weak positive range follows one formula is open, because the available formula does not reproduce single-unit changes even in strong jets | supports A11 for S1, S29 (90 % of range), S0 (with a gain change); weakens A5 (E7 z formula fails single-S interventions; 0.45 → 0.4); new A5n (0.55) |

Job e31-run4 1 CPU / 2 GiB, 1,682 s, peak 2,047 MiB; three CephFS-stalled nodes added to job.sh exclusions | Shift and turn are separable from real jets through the centroid offset; natural jets cannot separate m from √(M0M2). Splits at ranks 13/21 and soft additions are not separable on jet observables and their rates hold. **Conclusion:** (corrected by E31v) Shifted and turned jets differ from real jets only in quantities measured about the stored jet axis, and real jets never have a centroid offset large enough to change their mass by more than 0.7 %, so in real data the jet mass and the network's axis-moment mass are the same number. The result that JEDI-linear reads the spread about the axis rather than the mass therefore describes how it responds to axis-displaced inputs. The collinear-split, soft-particle and boost results hold on edited jets that lie among real jets | A4 scope: M2-vs-m is a property of the response to axis-displaced inputs (value unchanged, scope note); supports A15 (0.65 → 0.7) and A14 (on-manifold); new A4o (0.75) |

A direction-dependent part odd in c (rms 0.46 of the even part vs 0.14 predicted from M2) is present; C2 could not detect it. Only 70 % of the T1 margin-response variance lies along the dilation response (21 % on 0.044 towers). R falls toward the null at 1–2 mrad. Re-axised jets are among real jets on axis-free observables only. **Conclusion:** When the jet axis is computed from particles snapped to trigger-sized calorimeter towers, JEDI-linear changes its W/Z decision about 0.9 as much as it would for a real mass change of the predicted size, and this goes away when the spread about the new axis is restored, so the network reads the spread about whatever axis it is given. It also responds to the direction of the offset, by about half as much as the mass-like part, so the axis error is not seen purely as a mass change. How close this tower model is to a real L1 trigger (no threshold, pileup or seed-tower position) is untested | refines A4 (realistic-axis scope); new A4t (0.6); A4d 0.6 → 0.65; A4o note (on-manifold on axis-free observables only) |

The "hardest-particle pT sum" does not recur as a rank window: in the reference, HGQ s0, n128 and perminv the matched unit responds equally per GeV to pT added outside the window (ref 1.03 [0.76, 1.34]), so it tracks total jet pT; a genuine hard-particle window appears in 3 networks with different k. Mass-window recurrence not established. **Conclusion:** Across ten trained versions of JEDI-linear, two of the reference's jet quantities show up again in most of the quantized networks: the momentum-weighted squared distance of particles from the axis, and a count of soft particles. The reference's "sum of pT of the hardest particles" turns out to track the total jet pT, and a true hardest-particles window appears in only three networks, with different sizes. Whether these quantities recur more often than any formula fitted to the reference would is not settled with the number of decoy formulas tried | refines A1x (v2, 0.55 → 0.5); A3 note (S0's whole response is total-pT-like; rank window = its direct term); A16 note (circuit95 68–83 % of live units in quantized nets, not rerun) |

Responses are sign-asymmetric (mass −5 % 1.67 ×, girth −5 % 0.78 × the +5 % response). JEDI/MLP ratios shift with MLP refits (count/MLP_N 0.54 → 0.70). The untrained network responds to the radial mass edit as much as the trained one in SD units. **Conclusion:** JEDI-linear's quark-versus-gluon score moves when only the number of soft particles, the jet's width at fixed spread, the momentum concentration pT^D, or the radial spread is changed, each by roughly half to three quarters of what a classifier trained on these four variables does. More particles push toward gluon, more concentrated momentum toward quark. Verifier rerun (E36v, sample(300, 3701) minus overlaps, N 1,371, 778 edits; jobs e36-verify, e36-verify2, 1 CPU / 2 GiB): transfer 40.5 % [37.0, 44.2] vs random 1.88 %; at angle unit 0.05 rad 40.5 % vs 1.54 %; W→Z edits radial 0.45–0.47 vs 0.26 random, pT 0.41–0.43 vs 0.49; original hardest particle median +38.8 % (E36's +83 % = leading pT after re-sorting); sign-randomised 3.1 %, negated 1.4 %, pT part only 19.2 %, angle part only 3.3 %; hardness edit (leading pT × 2, pT^D +1.37 sd) flips 3.7 % [2.6, 4.9] vs random 1.1 %, rank-5 control 1.2 % (McNemar 30:3); never-flip baseline agreement 0.590, Cohen κ E11 0.114 [0.081, 0.147], B6 0.088, MLP 0.018. | E37 | A4s, A4, A10 (interchange weakness), A1x-type recurrence → new A4s v2 (loop A round 17) | where in JEDI-linear does the W/Z response to a more lopsided two-prong momentum split at fixed jet four-momentum live: does a low-dimensional learned subspace reproduce it under interchange, is that subspace separate from the mass route, which jet-shape quantity does it read, and does the response recur in other trained JEDI-linear networks? Network: official 3-feature n64, exact fixed point. Edit D(dc) (E35 = H45 edit_one): the jet is split into two exclusive-kT prongs; all particles are boosted to the jet rest frame, rotated together so that \|cos θ*\| (θ* = angle between the prong axis and the jet flight direction in the rest frame; 0 = equal momentum sharing, near 1 = one prong carries almost all) changes by dc ∈ {−0.3, −0.15, +0.15, +0.3}, and boosted back; jet four-momentum (so m, pT, axis), both prong masses and N fixed (max relative change 6e-13); dc > 0 = more lopsided. Controls: cn (same rest-frame angle about the prong axis: prong four-momenta and sharing fixed, comparable particle movement) and dilation (all angles × 0.95 / 1.05, a real mass change of about ∓5 %; positive control for the mass route). Fit = W+Z jets of sample(2000, 3701) (4,000 jets; 16,000 self + 7,946 cross pairs); test = W+Z of sample(2000, 3702) minus 369 shared rows (N 3,846; 15,384 self + 3,820 cross pairs). Arm 1, DAS (distributed alignment search: learn an orthonormal k-dim subspace Q of the live units of a layer such that the interchange h′ = h_b + QQᵀ(h_s − h_b), base = unedited jet, source = its own D(dc) version, followed by the exact fixed-point tail, reproduces the source's W−Z margin; 400 Adam steps, MSE) at S, h4, h5 and k ∈ {1, 2, 3, 4, 6}. Metrics: frac = share of the average margin change reproduced (cell-pooled over dc × class), IIA (interchange-intervention accuracy) = share of call-changing pairs where the patched W/Z call equals the call on the edited jet; 1,000 jet-bootstrap CIs. Nulls: 1,000 random live subspaces (rank p, Holm over 15 stage × k), shuffled-target DAS, DAS on 3 untrained redraws. Specificity: same Q on cn and dilation pairs; principal angles (arccos of the singular values of QᵀM) to refitted M0/M2 DAS subspaces and the dilation direction vs random. Arm 2: coordinate change regressed on Δz_min (softer prong's pT fraction), ΔD2 (β = 1), Δτ21 (hardest-particle axes), prong masses, pT^D, leading fraction; calibrated against the natural partial slope across unedited jets; steering h4 + δv vs D per unit coordinate, 200 random directions. Arm 3: D(±0.3) − cn on the 10 trained E33 networks and 10 untrained redraws (test W+Z, N 4,000); sign test, Fisher exact, permutation over networks, McNemar per network (Holm 20) | done; verified (E37v reproduced the average share and the mass specificity; the steering reading refuted for the k = 1 direction; "carries most of the response" holds for interchange (sufficiency) only; confidence 0.35) | Accuracy (all classes, stored inputs) 80.59 % on 3702, 80.21 % on 3701 (0.4 points below the 80.6–81.3 % band, binomial s.e. 0.4 %). Response (test, logits): D(+0.3) W −1.58, Z +1.63; cn(+0.3) +0.07 / +0.11; dilation × 1.05 −1.96 / −0.89. DAS on held-out self pairs (n 1,610 changed calls): h4 k = 1 frac 0.83 [0.80, 0.85] vs random mean 0.07 (q95 0.23), IIA 0.41 [0.38, 0.43] vs random 0.10 (q95 0.18), p 0.001, Holm 0.015; h4 k = 3 frac 0.84, IIA 0.41 (Holm 0.026); h5 k = 1 frac 0.66, IIA 0.49 (Holm 0.015); S within the random band at every k (k = 1 IIA 0.49 vs random q95 0.57, Holm 0.93). IIA flat in k (h4: 0.41, 0.32, 0.41, 0.45, 0.46). Shuffled-target DAS h4 k = 1 frac 0.43, IIA 0.46. Per-jet slope of patched on full change 0.55. Per dc at h4 k = 1: frac 0.91 (+0.3), 0.54 (−0.3). Cross pairs (n 328): IIA 0.29 vs random 0.15 (p 0.014). Specificity: dilation transfer 0.004 [0.001, 0.006] (random mean 0.014); max cosine with the M2 subspace 0.086 (random median 0.16), with the dilation direction 0.045. Read-out: h4 k = 1 coordinate joint R² 0.65; D2 calibrated slope 1.26 [1.11, 1.45] with matched-change ratio 2.19; τ21 3.8 / 4.9; z_min 1.9 / 3.3 (no observable with both near 1). Steering along the h4 k = 2 read-out direction per unit: W +0.50, Z +0.18 (same sign). Recurrence dc +0.3 (D − cn): W < 0 and Z > 0 in 9/10 trained (W −0.81 to −2.23, Z +0.84 to +2.67; exception HGQ r150 s1 at 56.7 % accuracy) and 0/10 untrained (\|D − cn\| ≤ 0.021); Fisher one-sided p 6e-5; permutation p 1e-4; all 20 trained McNemar Holm p < 1e-10 (official n64 W 475:42); reversed at dc −0.3 in the same 9. **Verifier E37v** (fresh fit sample(1500, 3801) W+Z 2,757, test sample(1500, 3802) W+Z 2,724, all E37 rows removed; jobs e37-verify / -b / -c, 1 CPU / 2 GiB): accuracy 80.37 %; D(+0.3) W −1.57, Z +1.52; cn +0.04 / +0.12; dilation −1.93 / −1.02. Stored h4 k = 1: frac 0.85 [0.82, 0.89], IIA 0.37 [0.33, 0.40] (n 1,156), dilation frac 0.004 [0.002, 0.007]; random k = 1 (300) frac 0.05 (q95 0.21), IIA 0.08 (q95 0.15), p 0.003. Refit: frac 0.84, IIA 0.37, \|cos\| with stored 0.75; shuffled-target fit frac 0.47, IIA 0.49. Non-learned directions: mean displacement (h4 shift at dc +0.3 minus at −0.3) frac 0.96 [0.91, 1.00], IIA 0.20; PC1 frac 0.66, IIA 0.41. Positive control: M2 subspace transfers 0.88 of the dilation response, and 0.55 [0.52, 0.58] of D's average change (random k = 2 q95 0.33), mostly the Z cell at +0.3 (Z +1.77 vs target +1.52; W −0.46 vs −1.57). Hold (edited jet's coordinate set back to its unedited value, share of the response removed) on D(+0.3), W / Z: DAS k = 1 0.47 / −0.08; M2 0.12 / 0.03; random q95 0.07 / 0.08; holding M2 removes 0.90–1.06 of the dilation response, DAS k = 1 0.01–0.09. Steering along the k = 1 direction (offset 2.87): in D's sign W −2.25 [−2.31, −2.19], Z +1.49 [1.40, 1.57]; opposite sign W +0.48, Z −0.95; antisymmetric contrast A (the part of the W/Z response that reverses with the offset's sign) 2.58 vs random q95 1.86, p 0.01; at c = 0.5 A 1.55 vs 1.07, p 0.01; 94.5 % of random directions push W toward Z and Z toward W for some sign at large offsets. Untrained redraws give near-constant outputs (redraws 7, 8 exactly constant), so the 0/10 shows the response needs training, not that it is W/Z-specific. Files: interp/e37_sharing_das_jedi.py, e37_plot.py, e37_verify.py; results/jedi/e37_das.json, e37_das_Q.npz, e37_das_M.npz, e37_untr.json, e37_recur.json, e37_verify*.json, e37_das.png; inbox/merged/E37_report.md, E37_verify.md | Conclusion: one direction in JEDI-linear's fourth layer (h4) acts as a signed "sharing" coordinate: copying it from the edited jet reproduces about 0.85 of the average W/Z change caused by a more lopsided prong split, it carries almost none of a real mass change, and moving a jet along it pushes W jets toward Z and Z jets toward W. It is needed for about half of the W-jet response and none of the Z-jet response, which the network's mass subspace also reproduces, and the edit response recurs in 9 of 10 trained JEDI-linear networks. Which jet-shape quantity the coordinate computes is open (no observable gives a calibrated read-out) | refines (new A4s v2 0.5; A4s v1 0.6 kept; thesis prediction IIA ≥ 0.7 not met (0.37–0.41); recurrence ≥ 7/10 met; A10 interchange weakness measured) |

Main sample(1000, 3801) N 5,000 (all ≥ 8 particles), replicate sample(1000, 3802) minus overlaps N 4,900, reference pool sample(1000, 3804) N 4,790. Edit T(k→j, f): a fraction f ∈ {0.05, 0.10, 0.20} of the pT of the rank-k particle (rank = position in pT order, 0 = hardest) is moved to rank j, k, j ∈ {0..5, rest = ranks 6–63 pooled}; 42 cells × 3 fractions; then all particle angles change by the minimum-norm amount (Gauss–Newton on E36's four-momentum Jacobian) that restores the summed four-momentum, so m, pT and the E-scheme axis are unchanged (compliance \|Δm\|/m < 1e-3, axis < 1 mrad: 98.9 %, ≥ 92 % per cell). Controls: (i) soft-sector null = random zero-sum pT reshuffle among ranks 6–63 with the same GeV L2 size, same projection; (ii) same fraction moved at the same rank distance inside ranks 14–40; (iii) rigid rotation + projection; (iv) 10 untrained redraws; (v) decomposition into the pT transfer alone (mass not restored) and the projection's angle moves alone. Metrics: change of the W−Z logit margin on W+Z jets and the q−g margin on q+g jets, 1,000 jet-bootstrap CIs; McNemar vs (i), Holm over 126 cells; ΔS/sd for 19 live S units. Naming: ridge on hinge bases of leading-particle features (pT_0, pT_0 + pT_1, pT_0 − pT_1, top-3 sum, pT^D, z_lead = pT_0/pT_J) vs radial features, edit slope on held-out edits (pass if CI in [0.8, 1.2]). A rise on the prong-sharing edit (κ 0.30 → 0.44) is reproduced almost entirely by adding rotated copies of ordinary jets, which carry no edit the program can see, so it reflects the fit's sensitivity to added data, not information in the counterfactual labels. Files: interp/e37x_sharing_physics_jedi.py, e37x_plot.py, e37x_verify.py; results/jedi/e37x.json, e37x_run1.json, e37x.png, e37x_verify.json; inbox/merged/E37x_report.md, E37x_verify.md | Conclusion: the fourth-layer sharing coordinate follows how unevenly the jet's momentum is split between its two prongs: changing only the split moves it strongly, while changing the prong separation or stretching the jet by the same mass change moves it about 8 % as much, and shape variables that mix split and separation (D2, τ21) predict the wrong sign for the separation change. It also responds to how wide the prongs are, which accounts for part of its larger-than-natural slope (about 2 per unit of the natural relation). Pushing the coordinate directly inside the network moves Z jets below about 85 GeV toward Z and above it toward W, while W jets move toward Z up to about 95 GeV, so its effect depends on the true class as well as the mass; under the fixed-four-momentum edit the coordinate alone reproduces the Z-jet response but holding it does not remove it, so other directions carry that response too | refines (new A4s v3 0.45; A4s v2 0.5 note) |

Networks: E33x's 10 (official 3f n32 / n64 / n128, perm-inv n64, 16f n64, HGQ r150 s0/s1/s2, float s0/s1; HGQ s1 at 57 % reported but left out of pooled numbers). Map fit sample(2000, 3939) N 10,000; test sample(2000, 3940) minus 401 shared, N 9,599. Stitch: ridge affine map L_A → L_B in float64 (α on a 20 % hold-out), clip to B's per-unit fit range (HGQ quantizers wrap out-of-range values), B's exact fixed-point tail; 90 ordered pairs × {S, h4}. Controls: random affine map with the same singular values; untrained redraws of A (seeds 0–2) with their own ridge map; map fitted on shuffled rows; self-stitch. Edits (8 networks with 3-feature 64-slot input): T = Δη + 0.06 rad at the stored axis (W jets, W→Z flow); D = E35 prong sharing dc +0.3 at fixed four-momentum, Δ(ℓW − ℓZ) on W jets (DW) and Z jets (DZ); N = one 1 GeV particle at r 0.3 (q, g jets); K = (Δη, Δφ) of pT ranks 13 and 14 swapped. Inheritance index I = (r_stitch − r_B)/(r_A − r_B), pooled over pairs with \|r_A − r_B\| > 2 SE (1 = like donor A, 0 = like recipient B). 1,000-draw jet bootstrap, McNemar, sign-flip permutation trained vs untrained donor (Holm over 90 pairs) | done; verified (E39v reproduced same-call on fresh jets and added a logit-only donor control; edit-inheritance reading refuted by network-level uncertainty and the 0.5 null; confidence 0.35) | Official n64 acc 80.43 % [79.57, 81.20]. Files: interp/e39_stitch_retrains_jedi.py, e39_plot.py, e39_verify.py, e39_verify_plot.py; results/jedi/e39.json, e39_run1.json, e39_stitch.png, e39_verify.json, e39_verify.png; inbox/merged/E39_report.md, E39_verify.md | Conclusion: any two separately trained JEDI-linear networks can be joined at the object layer or the next layer by one fitted linear map, and the joined network then gives the receiving network's decision on about 86–89 % of jets, close to how often the two networks agree on their own (90 %); with an untrained first half it is 34 %, and with only the first network's final class scores passed across it is 66–78 %. So the layer's usable content is linearly shared across retrains and carries more than the first network's answer; it is not shown that the networks compute in the same way. How the joined network responds to Z-jet prong sharing is set by the receiving network; the other edit responses cannot be assigned to either network with only 7 networks | new A1s 0.55 (refines A1x / A3 retrain stability) |

Conclusion: Formulas found by symbolic regression on standard jet variables reproduce the networks' decisions on ordinary jets almost as well as the if-statement program, but not how those decisions change when the jet is shifted. Other fits on the same standard variables fail the same way, so what matters is which variables a surrogate is given, not how the formula is searched for. Conclusion: every trained tagger changes 1–5 % of its calls when its hardest particle is replaced by two exactly collinear pieces, while the IRC-safe energy-flow network changes none; splitting more particles or removing the softest one shifts calls along the particle count (toward gluon or QCD with more particles). None of the taggers is infrared safe, but the soft-particle test uses particles softer than any in the data. PFN and EFN respond at the side-effect level, so their mW use is not separated from the edit's side effects (subjet shrinkage, lower tau21). The edit does not hold subjet masses fixed (docstring wrong). Moving top pairs onto 80.4 GeV from nearby changes no network and no MLPxW, so it does not separate the readings. Conclusion: changing the W-candidate subjet-pair mass inside top jets, with total mass and τ32 held fixed, lowers the top score of ParT, ParticleNet and LorentzNet about as much as a classifier given the W-pair mass, and 2–4 times more than a classifier that sees only the edit's side effects. These three Top-Landscape taggers use the W mass inside the top, unlike JEDI-linear on hls4ml (D1). Conclusion: trained quark–gluon taggers (ParT, ParticleNet, LorentzNet) do not count particles: adding a realistic soft particle or removing the softest one changes their output by at most about 0.03 in logit, while a count-only likelihood ratio moves 0.1. Splitting the hardest particle into two collinear pieces moves them toward gluon whether or not the count changes, so they are collinear unsafe through how the hard particles share energy. Natural-jet ADO cannot tell m from m_A; a surrogate with m_A reproduces JEDI's WTA response (Z/W 37.7 vs 37.6 %) and most of its translation response, and gives ParT responses of the same order but larger (r 0.3–0.6). Selected sets beyond the first observable vary with the sample; redrawn nulls only test binary-score sensitivity. Conclusion: stand-ins built from standard jet shapes rank W/QCD and top/QCD jets like each network in 92–97 % of pairs but never respond to the jet-axis convention, because they are axis-free by construction. Writing the jet mass as the mass about the network's given axis gives an equally good stand-in that reproduces JEDI-linear's axis response (37.7 vs 37.6 % of Z/W calls), so that response is mostly one known mass variable. The collinear-split and pT-scaling responses and ParT's Z vs W decision are not explained by either stand-in | supports D4, D4m (JEDI); new D8 (0.5); thesis note "beyond the IRC-safe literature" overstated |

For JEDI-linear 62 % of B-changed calls are W/Z → t, the D1 ΣpT shortcut; at the W/Z boundary Δ_B = Δ_P + Δ_A with P and A in opposite mass directions (the "reversed mass ordering" reading is withdrawn). EFN and ptnorm B = A follows from their pT-fraction inputs (not a finding); only ptflat bears on weakness 7. JetClass ParT/PN change 1.5–4× fewer calls under B than under P or A (full models 1.5–1.9× vs P), 2.5–9× more than R; Top ParT/PN/LorentzNet 3–4× fewer than P/A although trained in a narrow 550–650 GeV window, so "training-pT width or four-vector input" is not supported as the cause of the hls4ml-vs-Top difference. Absolute rates are not comparable across datasets. m_A changes up to 0.71 % (hls4ml) to 13 % (JetClass) per jet under B (mean ≤ 1e-4), not "≤ 0.12 %". Conclusion: boosting a jet along its own axis keeps its invariant mass (and its axis-moment mass) fixed but changes a third of JEDI-linear's calls (33 % vs 2.4 % for a matched rotation) and those of every other hls4ml-trained tagger; most of JEDI-linear's changed calls are W or Z jets becoming top, which also happens when only pT is lowered, i.e. the known ΣpT shortcut. JetClass and Top Landscape taggers change several times fewer calls under the boost than under pT-only or angle-only scaling. Why the hls4ml and Top taggers differ is not known: both were trained in narrow pT windows on equivalent particle inputs | new D9 (0.55); refines D1 (H3 boost was a first-order mass-preserving boost; exact pT-only gives W 87 → 48 %); weakness 7 only partly addressed (ptflat) |

Conclusion: pileup-like soft particles lower every network's signal efficiency faster than a soft-drop mass tagger, and the small axis shift they cause explains almost none of it. JEDI-linear reacts to the mass they add (one particle with the same mass and pT gives W efficiency 39 % vs 43 %), whereas the particle-ID JetClass networks and the Top Landscape ParT, LorentzNet and PFN react mainly to how many soft particles are added, even at the jet centre. Jobs d-h18-{qg,jc,top,hls,hlss}, d-h18-ref-*, d-h18-stats2, d-h18-verify; interp/h18_softcount_cross.py, interp/h18_verify.py; results/physics/h18_summary.json, h18_verify_summary.json; figure results/physics/h18_softcount.png | Conclusion: soft particles that add energy flow move every tagger toward the high-multiplicity class, and for quark–gluon networks the move is about that of an IRC-safe network that cannot count. When only the count changes (an exact collinear split of soft particles), quark–gluon networks move by 0.1–0.3 of the count-only likelihood ratio and JetClass W-vs-QCD networks by 1.2–2.3 times it, so whether a network uses the count depends on the model and training data, not on the edit. H18's single-particle (e1) comparison does not show this: the added particle had about 0.1 GeV in QG jets and about 1 GeV in JetClass jets. Open: whether the JetClass split halves (about 0.8 GeV) lie below the soft range the JetClass networks were trained on, and why top taggers move toward QCD while the count reference moves toward top. Density-ratio weight w(x) = p_TL(x)/p_gen(x) per jet from a Platt-calibrated HistGB on 24 jet features (V0 vs Top Landscape test rows T1, rng 6611), fitted on one half and applied to the other; check by C2ST (classifier two-sample test; AUC 0.5 = indistinguishable) against disjoint rows T2. Weighting a pair by its V0 jet alone, w(V0), selects pairs where V0 fluctuated toward Top Landscape (regression to the mean: ΔN 4.48 → 7.58 objects), so the main weight is pair-symmetric, w_sym = √(w(V0)·w(Hz+)). Metric Δs = weighted mean change in top log-odds per pair (Hz+ − V0), 2,000-pair bootstrap; count arm = V0 split / merged to the Hz+ object count at fixed IRC-safe content, rest = Hz+ − count arm (H0 rest = 0, sign-flip permutation, Holm over 14). Controls: IRC-safe EFN, six weight-permuted ParT copies, jitter (8 objects moved 0.005, N fixed). Data reference b_N: logistic regression of the true label on N with one intercept per (m, τ32, mW) cell (640 cells), 120k Top Landscape training jets. | H67 | D25 → D25 v2; D9, A4 (loop D round D14, exploratory) | does a tagger read the jet's invariant mass m, or its pT-weighted angular width w = Σ pT_i ΔR_i / Σ pT_i together with pT? Exact per-jet edits about the E-scheme axis (summed constituent 3-momentum), number of particles kept: prongs = exclusive-kT subjets (3 for top-class jets, 2 otherwise); two levers, prong pT sharing f (pT moved between prongs, prong centroids and Σ pT fixed) and prong opening u (prong centroids × u from the axis), then re-centring on the axis and rescaling to the original jet pT; a per-jet 2-D Newton solve hits the targets. E_w: w × (1 ± 0.1 / 0.2) at fixed m and pT; E_m: m × (1 ± 0.1) at fixed w and pT (achieved \|Δm/m\| < 1e-14, jet η, φ within 6e-8 in float64). The planned core ↔ edge lever inside prongs moves log m and log w almost equally (singular) and is kept only as a diagnostic. Controls: crot (each prong rotated about its centroid, re-solved to Δm = Δw = 0), crand (random in-prong pT shuffle of the same moved pT), TN (translation relative to the stored axis). Samples: hls4ml jedi_core.sample(2000, 6701); JetClass Wqq / Zqq / Tbqq / QCD 5,000 random rows each (rng 6702; 2,000 evaluated, 3,000 reference); Top Landscape test 5,000 + 5,000 (rng 6703) + 5,000 + 5,000 reference. The test cannot say which second quantity they use (width, prong sharing, D2 or τ21), because both edits change the prong sharing equally and classifiers on mass plus any one of these give similar responses. | H69 | D25 v2 → D25 v3 (loop D round D15) | at fixed jet invariant mass m and jet pT, which shape variable beside the mass do jet taggers read: the pT-weighted width w = Σ pT_i ΔR_i / Σ pT_i, the prong pT sharing s = 1 − Σ_k (P_k/P)² (P_k = pT of exclusive-kT prong k; 0 for one dominant prong, 0.5 for two equal prongs), D2 = e3/e2³ (ratio of the three-point to the cubed two-point energy correlator, β = 1) or τ21 = τ2/τ1 (N-subjettiness ratio)? Exact per-jet edits about the E-scheme axis (summed constituent 3-momentum), damped Newton solve with a finite-difference Jacobian (80 iterations); m, pT, η, φ exact (\|Δlog m\| ≤ 1e-8, \|Δlog pT\| ≤ 7e-16), particle count and order kept. Levers: f (pT moved between prongs), u (prong opening), c (within-prong pT reweighting pT_i × (1 + c h_i), h = distance to the axis orthogonalised to {1, Δη, Δφ, r²}, so each prong's pT, centroid and Σ pT r² stay fixed), cs (pT moved between core, within 0.05 of the prong axis, and edge of the softest prong). Edits: E_w (w ±5 / ±10 % at fixed m, pT, s), E_s (s ±5 / ±10 % at fixed m, pT, w), E_D2 (D2 ±5 / ±10 % at fixed m, pT, s), E_τ21 (τ21 ±10 % at fixed m, pT, s, D2). Controls: crot_dm (prong rotation with the edits' mean particle displacement, then m, w, s restored), crand (random per-particle displacement of equal size), one weight-permuted network per architecture. References trained on train rows and evaluated on the same edited jets: MLP(m,w,pT), MLP(m,s,pT), MLP(m,D2,pT), MLP(m,τ21,pT), GBDT on energy-flow polynomials (EFP). Samples: hls4ml jedi_core.sample(1000, 6901); JetClass val _122, 1,000 random rows each of Wqq, Zqq, Tbqq, Hbb, QCD (rng 6902); Top Landscape test 2,000 + 2,000 (rng 6903). Models JEDI-linear; JetClass ParT_full / kin, PN_full / kin; Top ParT, PN, LorentzNet, PFN, EFN. Δs = change of the own-class one-vs-rest log-odds; R = mean\|Δs(edit)\| / mean\|Δs(control)\|; φ_sD2 = M_s / (M_s + M_D2), M_X = mean \|Δs\| over the ± edits of X; bootstrap B 1000; paired sign-flip test (large-sample form); McNemar on call flips at ε_S = 0.5; Holm over 403 tests | done (verifier 0.45: reproduced; the headline R is unpaired (edit mean over converged jets ÷ control mean over all jets) and inflates the top-quark classes; crot_dm does not match the pT reweighting of the edits; "width cannot be edited on signal jets" refuted; corrected reading adopted) | Unedited accuracy: JEDI-linear 0.813 [0.802, 0.824] (known 0.807); ParT_full W / Z / t / QCD 0.795 / 0.705 / 0.955 / 0.781; ParT_kin 0.722 / 0.558 / 0.916 / 0.731; PN_full 0.774 / 0.659 / 0.928 / 0.767; PN_kin 0.695 / 0.532 / 0.904 / 0.695; Top ParT / PN / LorentzNet / PFN / EFN 0.948 / 0.946 / 0.950 / 0.932 / 0.921; permuted at chance (JEDI_perm0 constant). Fit Δs = a_κ ΔQ_κ through the origin per κ (quark jets, both generators, 79,952 jet-edit rows, 2-fold cross-fit by jet, held-out R²); κ* = the κ at which the soft-flip slope equals the hard-flip slope (if the network follows Q_κ*, soft and hard flips give the same response per unit ΔQ_κ*). Worker rerun seed 7611 (2k + 2k): unit vectors within 0.04. **Verifier H76v** (job d-h76-verify, GPU; seed 7621, 2k q + 2k g per generator; 83.8 / 80.1 % of jets pass every condition): unedited ParT_kin AUC 0.905, accuracy 0.829 (Py), 0.806 / 0.714 (Hw). ParT_kin vector (N, LHA, width, pT^D, λ(1,2)) per +10 %: (−0.010, −0.935, +0.703, −0.063, −0.223). Per-sign excess over crand at ±3 % (ParT_kin, Py): LHA+ −0.464 [−0.494, −0.435], LHA− +0.106 [0.086, 0.125], width+ +0.056 [0.036, 0.074], width− −0.360 [−0.383, −0.336]; even part (mean of the two signs) −0.179 (LHA), −0.152 (width). crand was size-matched to the mean of the two signs while LHA+ / width− are 2.5–3.4× larger; new control crand_big (full norm and per-particle profile of the larger edit, null space, C2ST 0.55–0.61) moves ParT +0.072 [0.057, 0.087]; LHA+ minus crand_big −0.479 [−0.509, −0.452], width− −0.345; calls changed 13.9 % vs 5.8 % (McNemar 383 / 111, p 5e-36). Unconstrained angularities move: LHA +3 % raises λ(1,3) by 12.8 % and λ(1,¼) by 2.5 %, lowers D = λ(1,1)/λ(1,½)² by 5.7 %. Cosine with ParT's 5-vector: EFN 0.996; GBDT on seven angularities λ(1, β = 0.1–3) + pT^D without N 0.973; GBDT with N 0.722; GBDT all five 0.498; single-variable GBDTs on r_1 (leading-particle radius) −0.990, D −0.968, λ(1,0.1) −0.967. Count edits ParT_kin: N+ (splits) −0.061 [−0.065, −0.058], N− (merges) −0.042; even −0.052 [−0.055, −0.049], odd −0.010; permuted ParT odd −0.051, even −0.001. pT^D both signs quark-ward (−0.084, −0.049), even −0.067. Holm 66/70 per generator. interp/h76_angularity_qg.py, h76_verify.py; results/physics/h76_run.json, h76_stats.json, h76_angularity.png, h76_verify.json (+ _v7611 files); inbox_d/merged/H76_report.md, H76_verify.md | Conclusion: when one classical quark/gluon angularity is moved with four others held fixed, the trained quark–gluon networks respond mostly to the edits that change how the jet's energy is spread in angle (LHA up or width down gives "quark"), in a pattern that matches the IRC-safe energy-flow network (cosine about 0.99) and that a size-matched random rearrangement does not reproduce. Changing the particle count or pT^D at fixed angularities moves them only in a way that does not depend on the direction of the change, so it is not counting. Still uncertain: which radial variable they read (a tree on angularities alone and single radial variables give the same pattern), the response is four to six times larger on one side, and the angular-spread edits produce jets a classifier separates from real ones (AUC about 0.87) | refines D7 v2 (0.75, note: no odd count response at fixed angularities) and D22 v2 (0.65, note: pT^D has a small partial effect at fixed angular shape); new D30 (0.3) |
